## v1.97.13

- Next item from the Technical Library audit Build list (TL-083 "Two-Wattmeter Calculator", division Electrical Measurements; the audit status was "Guide only", with the two-wattmeter method guide). New calculator `two-wattmeter` in division 32 Testing and Measurement (existing division, so the division count does not change). Two directions: from two signed wattmeter readings to the load, or from a total power, power factor and lagging or leading type to the two readings expected. It gives the total real power W1 + W2, the reactive power sqrt 3 (W1 - W2) (positive lagging, negative leading from the load type selected), the apparent power, the power angle, the power factor, the larger and smaller reading, the smaller to larger ratio, and the line current at a given line voltage.
- Conventions: the default (8 kW and 4 kW) reproduces the first worked example of the formula guide (12 kW, 6.928 kVAR, 30 degrees, 0.866) and the guide's 6 kW and -2 kW case gives 4 kW, 13.86 kVAR, 73.9 degrees and 0.2774. The angle is atan2(sqrt 3 |W1 - W2|, W1 + W2), so the order of the two readings does not matter. Readings from a load are (S / sqrt 3) cos(30 degrees -/+ phi). The calculator gives the larger and the smaller reading rather than naming the line, because that depends on the phase sequence. Warnings: a negative smaller reading (pf below 0.5, keep the sign) and a zero smaller reading (pf exactly 0.5). Rejected: readings that add to 0 or below, a total power of 0 or below, a power factor outside (0, 1] and a line voltage of 0 or below.
- 44 independent vectors in `tests/gen_vectors.py` (own random stream, earlier 4,998 unchanged): 18 random loads and 4 named, each in both directions. Written from phasors: a balanced ABC load is built from line voltage, line current and angle, each meter is Re(V conj I) with its current coil in one line and its voltage coil to line B, and the true totals come from the phase quantities. The engine agreed with every vector.
- 15 built-in self-tests, taken from the named vectors: the guide's two examples, equal readings, reversed order, a leading load, three load-to-readings cases (pf 0.8, 0.3 with a negative reading, 0.5 with a zero reading, 0.95 leading) and six rejections.
- Library card TL-083 links to the calculator. Guides linked: two-wattmeter, three-phase power, power factor, unbalance. `tools/calc_scope.json` has a checked and not-checked entry (unbalanced and four-wire loads, meter and transformer errors, harmonics and the sign of reactive power from the readings alone are not covered). Stage 3: classified Not applicable in `tools/standard_applicability.json` (textbook measurement theory; IEC 62053 and IEC 61010 are not applied), a scope screen to confirm; the registry now has 57 scope screened plus 141 Not applicable = 198. ## v1.97.16

- Next item from the Technical Library audit Build list (TL-097 "Switching Loss Comparison Tool", division Power Electronics; audit status "Guide only", with the MOSFET Loss Formulas guide). New calculator `switching-loss-comparison` in division 24 Semiconductor Components (existing division, so the division count does not change). It compares two devices, or three when device C is filled in, in the same stage: switched voltage, on-state current, duty cycle, switching frequency and gate voltage are shared, and each device has its on-resistance, rise time, fall time and total gate charge. Per device it gives the conduction loss I^2 Rds D, the switching loss 1/2 V I (tr + tf) fsw, the gate drive loss Qg Vgs fsw, the total, the loss inside the device (conduction plus switching), the share that is switching and gate loss, and the frequency where conduction equals switching plus gate loss. It names the device with the lowest total, the saving over the worst, and for each pair the break-even frequency where the totals are equal (f = (Pc,x - Pc,y) / (ky - kx) with k = 1/2 V I (tr + tf) + Qg Vgs), shown only when it is above 0.
- The default (device A: 10 mohm, 20 ns, 20 ns, 50 nC at 48 V, 10 A, 100 kHz, D 0.5, 10 V) reproduces the guide's worked example (0.5 W + 0.96 W + 0.05 W = 1.51 W) and its frequency table (50 kHz and 500 kHz); the default device B (4 mohm, 35 ns, 30 ns, 140 nC) loses 1.90 W and breaks even with A at 43.48 kHz. Outputs that do not apply (an unused device C, a pair with no break-even) are hidden, not shown as a dash. Rejected: a voltage, current, frequency, gate voltage, on-resistance, edge time or gate charge of 0 or below, a duty cycle outside (0, 1), a partly filled device C, and edge times that do not fit in the on or off time. Warnings: edge times over 10% of the period, a ranking that reverses at a frequency (the break-even), and gate loss larger than the loss inside the device.
- 55 independent vectors in `tests/gen_vectors.py` (own random stream, earlier 5,136 unchanged), written from the waveforms: each turn-on and turn-off energy is integrated numerically (voltage held while the current ramps, current held while the voltage ramps), conduction is I^2 Rds integrated over the on-time, gate energy is Vg times the charge, and the break-even and crossover frequencies are found by bisection on the loss difference. 44 random vectors (two and three devices) and 11 named, including the guide example; the engine agreed with every vector. 11 built-in reference cases (guide example and table rows, three devices, equal devices, six rejections) plus the generated unit and limit tests.
- Library card TL-097 links to the calculator; guides linked: MOSFET loss, heat sink, buck and boost converters, linear regulators. `tools/calc_scope.json` entry added. Stage 3: Not applicable in `tools/standard_applicability.json` (textbook loss relations; a scope screen to confirm). Not in `tools/team_review_approval.json`. Page title short name "MOSFET Switching Loss Comparison". The overlap estimate leaves out output capacitance and diode recovery loss; the page says so.

## v1.97.17

- Next item from the Technical Library audit Build list (TL-153 "Sampling / Resolution Calculator", division Communication & Signal Systems; audit status "Guide only", with the ADC Resolution and Sampling Formulas guide). New calculator `adc-sampling` in division 31 Instrumentation (existing division, so the division count does not change; the Sensor Range Converter and 4-20 mA Loop calculators are already there). Inputs: bits (whole number, 1 to 32), full-scale input range, the voltage at code 0 (so bipolar ranges work), an input voltage, a sensor signal span inside the range with its span in its own units, the highest signal frequency, the sampling rate and a measured SINAD. Outputs: step size (LSB), levels, highest code, the ideal count for the input voltage, maximum quantisation error, ideal full-scale SNR, ENOB and bits lost, counts across the sensor span, percentage of range used, resolution of the span in bits, one count as a percentage of the span, sensor units per count, the largest gain that keeps the span in range, Nyquist frequency, oversampling ratio, extra bits from oversampling, and the alias frequency of a tone above half the sampling rate. Outputs that do not apply (the oversampling gain at or below the Nyquist limit, the alias of a tone that is not aliased) are hidden.
- The default (12 bits, 3.3 V, 1.65 V in) reproduces the guide's first example (0.8057 mV, 2,048 counts, 74 dB) and a named case reproduces its 4 to 20 mA example (5 V reference, 1 V to 5 V span: 3,276.8 counts, 4.883 uA per count). The SNR and ENOB use the exact constants 6.0206 and 1.7609 where the guide shows 6.02 and 1.76, so the ideal 12-bit SNR is 74.01 dB and a SINAD of 68 dB gives an ENOB of 11.002.
- Rejected: a fractional bit count, a sensor span whose high value is at or below its low value or that lies outside the input range, a SINAD of 1.76 dB or less, and a range, frequency or span of 0 or below. Warned: an input outside the range (it would clip), a sampling rate at or below twice the highest frequency (with the alias), a rate under 2.5 times it (no room for filter roll-off), a SINAD that gives an ENOB above the bits, an ENOB more than 2 bits below the bits, and a sensor span using under 50% of the range.
- 45 independent vectors in `tests/gen_vectors.py` (own random stream, earlier 5,191 unchanged), written from the converter's behaviour: the count is found by bisection over the transition levels with exact fractions, the SNR is the power ratio of a full-scale sine to LSB^2 / 12 noise, the ENOB solves that relation for the bits, the alias is the tone folded about fs / 2, and the oversampling gain is the in-band noise power falling by the ratio. 40 random and 5 named; the engine agreed with every vector. 12 built-in reference cases (7 named, 5 rejections or warnings) plus the generated unit and limit tests.
- Library card TL-153 links to the calculator; guides linked: ADC resolution, 4-20 mA loop, RC filter, op-amp. `tools/calc_scope.json` entry added. Stage 3: Not applicable in `tools/standard_applicability.json` (textbook relations; IEEE 1241 and IEC 60748-4 converter test methods not applied), a scope screen to confirm. Not in `tools/team_review_approval.json`. Page title short name "ADC Resolution and Sampling". The calculator takes an ideal quantiser; offset, gain, non-linearity, jitter and the anti-alias filter are outside it, and the page says so.
- Counts: 202 calculators, 5,236 vectors, 174 calculator pages, 567 sitemap URLs. Engine and service-worker cache 1.97.17. No existing results changed.

## v1.97.18

- Next item from the Technical Library audit Build list (TL-176 "Load Factor Calculator", division Energy Management; audit status "Guide only", with the Demand, Load and Diversity Factor Formulas guide). New calculator `load-factor` in division 07 Power and Energy (existing division, so the division count does not change; Maximum Demand and Energy Audit are already there, but neither gives the load factor). Inputs: connected load, a demand factor, an optional measured maximum demand (which overrides connected load times demand factor), the energy used and the length of the period, and an optional sum of the individual maximum demands. Outputs: maximum demand, demand factor, average demand, load factor (also in percent), the peak above the average, the energy at the maximum demand for the whole period, the share of it unused, and the diversity factor (hidden when no sum is entered).
- The default reproduces the guide's worked example (500 kW at 0.7 = 350 kW; 168,000 kWh in 720 h = 233.3 kW average; load factor 0.6667; diversity factor 1.2 from 420 kW). Rejected: an average demand above the maximum demand (load factor above 1), and a connected load, demand factor, energy, period or maximum of 0 or below, or a demand factor above 1. Warned: a load factor under 40% or over 90%, a diversity factor below 1, a sum of maxima above the connected load, and a maximum demand above the connected load.
- 24 independent vectors in `tests/gen_vectors.py` (own random stream, earlier 5,236 unchanged), written from metered demand profiles: random half-hourly profiles per feeder over 1, 7 or 30 days, energy by summing intervals, maximum as the largest interval of the summed profile, diversity as the sum of the feeder maxima over it; one in three leaves the measured maximum blank. The engine agreed with every vector. 8 built-in reference cases (5 named, 1 rejection, 3 warnings; some cases count in both) plus the generated unit and limit tests.
- Library card TL-176 links to the calculator; guides linked: demand and load factor, energy and cost, energy meter multiplier, transformer. `tools/calc_scope.json` entry added. ## v1.97.19

- Next item from the Technical Library audit Build list (TL-022 "RC/RL Time Constant Calculator", division Electrical Circuit Theory; audit status "Partial": the existing `rc-time-constant` gives τ = RC and the cutoff frequency only, with no RL time constant and no step response). New calculator `rc-rl-transient` in division 03 AC Circuits (existing division, so the division count does not change; the second-order `rlc-transient` is already there). Four cases: RC capacitor charging from 0 V, RC capacitor discharging from a starting voltage, RL voltage step from zero current, and RL current decaying through R from a starting current. Inputs: R, C (RC cases), L (RL cases), the source or starting voltage, the starting current (RL decay), a time, and a level in percent. Outputs: time constant (RC or L/R), corner frequency 1/(2πτ), starting and final value, output at the chosen time (capacitor voltage or inductor current), share of the change completed, the companion value (resistor current for RC, inductor voltage for RL, magnitude), stored energy, the time to complete the chosen percentage, the half-time (0.693 τ), the 10 to 90% time (2.197 τ) and the 99% time (4.605 τ).
- The default is 1 kΩ with 1 µF (and 1 H, which also gives τ = 1 ms), 10 V, read at 2 ms: 8.647 V, 86.47% complete. Rejected: a resistance, capacitance or inductance of 0 or below, a negative time, and a level of 0% or less or 100% or more. Warned: a chosen time beyond five time constants (more than 99% complete). The RL decay case models a decay through R only; an inductor opened with no path is not modelled (stated in the assumptions).
- 40 independent vectors in `tests/gen_vectors.py` (own random stream, earlier 5,260 unchanged), written from the circuit differential equations: C dv/dt = (Vs − v)/R or L di/dt = Vs − R i integrated with fourth-order Runge-Kutta, the time constant read from the initial slope, the 50%, 10 to 90%, 99% and chosen-level times found by bisection on the integrated trajectory, the companion value from C dv/dt or L di/dt, and the energy from the state. 10 random cases of each of the four events. The engine agreed with every vector. 7 built-in reference cases (4 named, 1 warning, 2 rejections) plus the generated unit and limit tests.
- Library card TL-022 links to the calculator; guides linked: RC filter, capacitor discharge, inductor, thermal time constant. `tools/calc_scope.json` entry added. Stage 3: Not applicable in `tools/standard_applicability.json` (textbook circuit identity; a scope screen to confirm). Not in `tools/team_review_approval.json`. Page title short name "RC and RL Time Constant". `rc-time-constant` is unchanged.

New calculator `magnetic-flux-density` in division 04 DC Circuits (existing division, next to the Magnetic Circuit Calculator, so the division count does not change). Four ways to find B: from flux and area, from field strength H and relative permeability, from a coil or solenoid (turns, current, length), and from a long straight conductor (current and distance). Warned: a medium with μr above 1 taken above 1.6 T (saturation). A constant μr is used; the B-H curve of real iron is not (stated in the assumptions).

## v1.97.21

- Next item from the Technical Library audit Build list (TL-036 "Motor Efficiency Calculator", division Electrical Machines; audit status "Partial": the existing `motor-power-output` finds shaft output and loss from a measured input and a stated efficiency, with no loss split, no efficiency at part load and no way to read the load). New calculator `motor-efficiency-loss` in division 12 Motors (existing division, so the division count does not change). It uses the two-part loss model: a fixed loss (core, friction and windage) plus a loss that rises with the square of the load, together equal to Pr (1/ηr - 1) at full load. Inputs: rated shaft power, full-load efficiency, the fixed share of the full-load loss (or a measured no-load input power, which replaces the share), the way to find the load, operating hours, a tariff and an optional replacement-motor efficiency. Three ways to find the load: a percentage you enter, a measured input power (solved from the loss model), or the measured speed by the slip method x = (Ns - N) / (Ns - Nr). Outputs: shaft output, input, total loss, efficiency and load at that point, the fixed and load-dependent losses, efficiency at 100, 75, 50 and 25% load, the load and value of peak efficiency (x = sqrt(Pf / Pv)), the energy drawn and lost over a year, the cost of the losses, and (hidden when no replacement efficiency is entered) the input power, energy and money saved by a replacement at the same shaft load.
- The default (55 kW, 93% at full load, 35% fixed share, read at 75% load) gives 41.25 kW out, 44.213 kW in, 93.30% efficiency and a peak of 93.30% at 73.4% load; the formula guide's 55 kW, 6,000 h, 91% to 94% replacement at 8 per kWh reproduces its 1.929 kW and 11,570 kWh (92,590 in money, rounded in the guide). Rejected: a full-load efficiency of 0 or below or 100% or more, a fixed share outside (0, 100), a no-load input at or above the full-load loss, a measured input at or below the fixed loss or needing more than 150% load, a rated or measured speed at or above synchronous, and a slip-method load above 150%. Warned: load below 40% (light load), above 100% (overload, model used beyond its rating), and a replacement that is not more efficient.
- 45 independent vectors in `tests/gen_vectors.py` (own random stream, earlier 5,340 unchanged), written from the loss model: the load of peak efficiency is found by mpmath root-finding on d(eta)/dx (30 digits), the load from input power by bisection on Pin(x), the slip load in exact fractions; for the input-power case the input is built from a chosen load and the engine must recover it. 15 vectors of each way, a third with a no-load input power and a third with no replacement. Tolerance 1e-7. The engine agreed with every vector. 15 built-in reference cases (9 named, 6 rejections) plus the generated unit and limit tests.
- Library card TL-036 links to the calculator; guides linked: motor efficiency, motor formulas, motor temperature rise, energy cost. `tools/calc_scope.json` entry added. Stage 3: Not applicable in `tools/standard_applicability.json` (textbook loss model; IEC 60034-2-1 and IEC 60034-30-1 test methods and IE classes not applied), a scope screen to confirm. Not in `tools/team_review_approval.json`. Page title short name "Motor Efficiency and Load". `motor-power-output` is unchanged. The fixed share is an estimate unless a no-load test is entered, and the page says so.

## v1.97.23

- Next item from the Technical Library audit Build list (TL-089 "Boolean Logic Tool", article "Logic Gates, Boolean Algebra and Truth Tables", division Electronics Engineering; audit status "Partial": `binary-calculator` and `number-base-converter` existed, with no Boolean or truth-table tool). New calculator `boolean-logic` in division 23 Electronics (existing division, so the division count does not change). For 2, 3 or 4 inputs it takes a standard gate (AND, OR, NAND, NOR, XOR, XNOR or majority) or any function given as its truth-table number (bit 0 is the output for all inputs 0, A is the most significant bit of the row number; 0 to 15, 255 or 65535). It gives the truth table, the output at a chosen row, the minterm and maxterm counts, the truth-table numbers of F and of its complement, the prime implicants and the essential ones, and the minimal sum of products and product of sums with their term and literal counts. The steps list the table four rows to a line, the minterms and maxterms as Σm and ΠM, the prime implicants (essential ones starred) and both minimal expressions.
- The default is the 3-input majority function (truth-table number 232) read at A = 1, B = 0, C = 1: F = 1, minimal SOP AB + AC + BC (3 terms, 6 literals), minimal POS (A + B)(A + C)(B + C). Minimal means fewest terms, then fewest literals; the counts are exact (an exhaustive search over the prime implicants), and when two or more forms tie one is shown and a warning says so (the six-cycle function, number 231, has two). Constant functions and functions that do not depend on every input are flagged. The product of sums is the minimal SOP of the complement turned by De Morgan; a SOP of a constant 1 is written 1 and of a constant 0 is written 0, and the POS follows the same convention. Rejected: a custom truth-table number that is blank, fractional, negative or above 2^(2^n) − 1 for the chosen number of inputs (the number is ignored for the standard gates).
- 48 independent vectors in `tests/gen_vectors.py` (own random stream, earlier 5,385 unchanged), written without the engine's cube enumeration: the primes come from the Quine-McCluskey tabular method on strings of 0, 1 and -, the essential primes from the coverage chart, and the cheapest cover from trying every subset of the primes, with the number of covers at the minimum cost counted. Each minimal SOP and each product of sums is evaluated on every row and must equal the truth table (asserted in the generator). 21 gate vectors (every gate at 2, 3 and 4 inputs), 24 random custom truth tables (8 per input count) and 3 named (232, 231, 18407). The engine agreed with every vector at the first run. 15 built-in reference cases (9 named, 1 tie warning, 5 rejections) plus the generated unit and limit tests.
- Library card TL-089 links to the calculator; guides linked: relay coils, ADC resolution, contactor sizing (the guides are the nearest formula articles; there is none on logic). `tools/calc_scope.json` has a checked and not-checked entry (no don't-care rows, no multi-level or NAND-only mapping, no timing or hazards, no sequential logic). Stage 3: classified Not applicable in `tools/standard_applicability.json` (textbook Boolean algebra and two-level minimisation; IEC 60617 and IEEE 91 give gate symbols, not the algebra), a scope screen to confirm. Not in `tools/team_review_approval.json`. Page title short name "Boolean Logic and Truth Table"; the typical-values table varies the truth-table number over 1, 105, 127, 128, 150, 232 and 254 (3-input NOR, XNOR, NAND, AND, XOR, majority and OR). `binary-calculator` and `number-base-converter` are unchanged.
- Counts: 207 calculators, 5,433 vectors, 179 calculator pages, 572 sitemap URLs. Engine and service-worker cache 1.97.23. No existing results changed.

It takes the module data (rated power, Voc, Vmp, Isc and the Voc and Vmp temperature coefficients), the modules per string and number of strings, the lowest and highest cell temperature, and the inverter data (AC rating, maximum DC voltage, MPPT window, number of MPPT inputs, short-circuit current limit per input, efficiency), with an assumed peak array output as a fraction of the STC rating and a target DC to AC ratio. It gives the array rating, the DC to AC ratio, the inverter rating for the target ratio and the largest array for the inverter at that ratio, the cold string Voc with the most modules allowed, the hot and cold string Vmp, the strings and short-circuit current on the busiest MPPT input, the AC output at the assumed peak, the inverter loading and clipped share at that instant, and the array output at which the inverter reaches its rating.

Rejected: non-whole string length, strings or MPPT inputs, Vmp not below Voc, an MPPT window that is inverted or above the maximum DC voltage, and a highest cell temperature not above the lowest.

The engine agreed with every vector at the first run. 9 built-in reference cases.

- Next item from the Technical Library audit Build list (TL-145 "Equipment Loss Calculator", article "Industrial Motor and Transformer Energy Loss Assessment", division Industrial Engineering; audit status "Partial": `transformer-efficiency`, `cable-power-loss`, `motor-efficiency-loss` and `motor-power-output` covered one item each, with no calculator that adds the losses of the three and prices them). New calculator `equipment-loss` in division 07 Power and Energy (existing division, so the division count does not change). It takes a transformer (rating, no-load loss, full-load loss, average loading, power factor, energised hours and hours at that loading), a motor (rated shaft output, full-load efficiency, fixed-loss share, shaft load and running hours) and a feeder cable (current, length, resistance, voltage, one-phase or three-phase and carrying hours), plus a tariff. It gives the transformer iron, copper and total loss and its efficiency at the loading; the motor fixed, load-dependent and total loss and its efficiency at the load; the cable loss and its share of the power it carries; the total loss in kW; the yearly energy lost by each item and in total in kWh; each item's share of the yearly loss; the share of the loss that is fixed (transformer iron loss and motor core and friction loss, present whenever the equipment is switched on); and the yearly cost. The step list names the largest contributor.
- The default is a 1,000 kVA transformer (1.7 kW iron, 10.5 kW copper) at 60% for 6,000 of 8,760 energised hours, a 55 kW motor (93% at full load, 35% fixed loss) at 75% for 6,000 h, and a 100 A, 200 m, 0.2 ohm/km three-phase cable for 6,000 h at 8 a kWh: transformer 5.480 kW (98.995%), motor 2.963 kW (93.30%), cable 1.200 kW (1.92% of the power it carries), 9.643 kW together; 37,572 + 17,775 + 7,200 = 62,547 kWh a year, of which the transformer is 60.1%, the motor 28.4% and the cable 11.5%, 37.7% of it fixed, costing 500,378. Warnings: transformer below 30% of rating (iron loss dominates), transformer above 100% (overload), motor below 40% load, motor above 100% (overload), cable loss above 5% of the power it carries (all practice guidelines, not standard requirements). Rejected: hours at the loading above the energised hours, a full-load efficiency or fixed share of 0 or 100% or more, and zero or negative ratings, currents, lengths, resistances or hours.
- 38 independent vectors in `tests/gen_vectors.py` (own random stream, earlier 5,583 unchanged), written as a different route from the engine's data-sheet scaling, with 30-digit mpmath: the transformer is a three-phase equivalent circuit (core resistance from the iron loss, series resistance from the copper loss and the rated phase current, evaluated at a chosen line voltage that drops out of the answer), the motor is a rated input whose loss is split into a fixed power and a winding loss in an equivalent resistance at a fictitious rated current, the cable is summed conductor by conductor, and the year is stepped hour by hour, adding the loss in force in each hour. 32 random and 6 named (the default, single-phase cable, a lightly loaded transformer, transformer and motor both overloaded, everything running all year, a long heavily loaded cable). The engine agreed with every vector at the first run. 11 built-in reference cases, including the cable length entered in km. The same author wrote the engine and the vectors, so the independence is in the method.
- Library card TL-145 links to the calculator; guides linked: transformer efficiency, motor efficiency, cable loss, energy and cost, transformer loss of life. `tools/calc_scope.json` has a checked and not-checked entry (no load profile, no harmonic, stray or temperature effects, no switchgear, busbar or capacitor losses, no Ecodesign or IEC limit or class check). Stage 3: classified Not applicable in `tools/standard_applicability.json` (loss accounting from user data; IEC 60076-1 and -7, IEC 60034-2-1 and -30-1 and IEC 60287 define how the data-sheet figures are measured, classed or rated, not this arithmetic), a scope screen to confirm. ## v1.97.29

- Next item from the Technical Library audit Build list (TL-149 "Calibration Error Calculator", article "Instrument Calibration: Range, Span, Accuracy and Traceability", division Instrumentation & Control; audit status "Partial": `percentage-error` compares one measured value with one true value, with no calibration table, as-found and as-left comparison, tolerance check or zero and span error). New calculator `calibration-error` in division 31 Instrumentation (existing division, so the division count does not change). It takes the instrument range (low and high values), a tolerance as a percentage of span, an optional reference-standard accuracy as a percentage of span, and three, four or five test points, each with the applied value as a percentage of span, the as-found reading and the as-left reading, all in the range unit. It gives every point's as-found and as-left error as a percentage of span, the largest as-found error (as a percentage of span and in the range unit) and the point it falls on, the largest as-left error, how many points are outside tolerance as found and as left, the zero error and span error of the as-found points from a least-squares straight line (error at 0% of span, and the change in error from 0% to 100%), the error that would be left after a zero and span adjustment (the largest distance of a point from that line), and the test uncertainty ratio (tolerance over reference accuracy). Unused point rows and the ratio (when the reference accuracy is 0) are left out of the results, not shown as dashes. An error equal to the tolerance passes, with a 1e-9 guard against floating-point noise.
- The default is a 0 to 10 range with a 0.5% tolerance and a 0.05% reference on five points (0, 25, 50, 75 and 100% of span): as found the errors are 0.2, 0.4, 0.8, 1.2 and 1.4% of span, so three points are outside tolerance (largest 1.4% at point 5), with a zero error of 0.16% and a span error of 1.28% and 0.08% of span left after a zero and span adjustment; as left the largest error is 0.1% and none is outside; the test uncertainty ratio is 10 : 1. Warnings: points outside tolerance as found (the instrument had drifted), points outside tolerance as left (the adjustment failed), left-over error after a zero and span adjustment larger than the tolerance (non-linear characteristic or damaged element), and a test uncertainty ratio below 4 : 1 (a common practice guideline, not a requirement of the method). Rejected: a range high value not above the low value, a zero or negative tolerance, an applied value outside 0 to 100%, and test points that are all at one applied value (the line needs two).
- 39 independent vectors in `tests/gen_vectors.py` (own random stream, earlier 5,621 unchanged), 34 random and 5 named (the default, a temperature range of -50 to 150, a weak reference giving 2.5 : 1, a bowed curve, and an error exactly at the tolerance), written as a different route from the engine's closed forms, with 30-digit mpmath and exact fractions: each reading is first placed on a normalised 0 to 1 span scale and the error is 100 times the difference of the two fractions, the tolerance test is an exact fraction comparison, and the zero and span error come from a QR decomposition of the design matrix [1, p] with back substitution, not from sums of squares. 14 built-in reference and rejection cases. The engine agreed with every vector at the first run, except one case in which the reference carried 1e-40 of rounding noise where the exact span error is zero; the generator now treats a fit value below 1e-25 as exactly zero. The same author wrote the engine and the vectors, so the independence is in the method.
- Library card TL-149 links to the calculator; guides linked: percentage error, measurement uncertainty, 4-20 mA loop. `tools/calc_scope.json` has a checked and not-checked entry (no tolerance as a percentage of reading, no hysteresis or repeatability separation, no calibration uncertainty budget, no guard band, no traceability check). Stage 3: classified Not applicable in `tools/standard_applicability.json` (textbook calibration arithmetic; ISO/IEC 17025 and JCGM 100 define uncertainty and traceability practice, not this arithmetic), a scope screen to confirm. Not in `tools/team_review_approval.json`. Page short name "Calibration Error and Tolerance". The calculator uses the engine's existing `na` list to leave unused outputs out; the engine's other behaviour is unchanged.

New calculator `electric-potential` in division 04 DC Circuits (existing division, so the division count does not change). It takes one, two or three point charges (signed, in nC to C or e) with their positions in a plane, the field point, the relative permittivity of the medium (1 or more) and a test charge. It gives the distance from each charge, each charge's potential kq/r, the total potential (a signed sum, since potential is a scalar), the work to bring the test charge from infinity (q0 times V), the electrostatic energy of the group (each pair counted once; left out for one charge) and the net charge. Warning: when the potentials cancel (zero potential is not zero field, shown for an equal and opposite pair on the midline). Rejected: the field point on a charge, two charges at one position, and a relative permittivity below 1.

The calculator agreed with every vector at the first run. 10 built-in cases (7 reference, 3 rejection); a deliberately wrong expected value was confirmed to fail, so the cases do compare values. The same author wrote the engine and the vectors, so the independence is in the method.

- Next item from the Technical Library audit Build list (TL-158 "Thermal Rise Estimator", article "Heat Transfer in Electrical Equipment and Enclosures", division Engineering Physics; audit status "Partial": `heatsink-thermal` and `water-heater-kw` cover a semiconductor heatsink and heating a liquid, with no general enclosure temperature-rise estimate). New calculator `thermal-rise` in division 29 Industrial Electrical (existing division, so the division count does not change). It takes the heat dissipated inside a sealed enclosure, the size and mounting (free-standing or wall-mounted) or an exposed area entered by hand, the surface material (painted steel 5.5, stainless 4.5, aluminium 12 or plastic 3.5 W/m²K, or an entered coefficient), the ambient and highest allowed internal air temperature, and optionally a thermal mass and a time. It gives the exposed area, the conductance G = hA, the steady-state rise and internal air temperature, the heat the enclosure can lose with the air at the limit, the excess heat to remove and the ventilation airflow that removes it (outside air in at ambient, out at the limit), and with a mass the time constant, the temperature after the stated time and the time to reach the limit. Results that do not apply (no excess, no mass, limit never reached) are left out through the engine's `na` list.
- The default is 300 W in a 0.8 × 1.8 × 0.4 m free-standing painted steel enclosure (4.64 m², 25.52 W/K) at 35 °C ambient with a 45 °C limit: rise 11.76 K, internal air 46.76 °C (1.76 K over the limit), 255.2 W lost at the limit, 44.8 W to remove, 13.37 m³/h of air; with 150 kg at 500 J/kg·K the time constant is 49.0 min, the air is 45.74 °C after 2 h and reaches the limit after 93.1 min. The warning says the enclosure cannot lose the heat by itself. Rejected: an internal limit at or below the ambient (sizes, area and coefficient must be above zero through the input limits). The material coefficients are typical figures for enclosures in cooling guides, not values from a standard, and the page says so; the formal assembly temperature-rise method (IEC 61439-1 and IEC TR 60890, with its own area and installation factors) is not applied, which the notes and the scope page state.
- 34 independent vectors in `tests/gen_vectors.py` (own random stream, earlier 5,694 unchanged), 30 random and 4 named (the default, wall-mounted aluminium inside the limit, no thermal mass, and an area and coefficient entered by hand with the rise exactly at the limit), written as a different route from the engine's closed forms, with 20-digit mpmath: the area is the sum of a list of faces, the rise is heat times a thermal resistance, the airflow goes through a mass flow, and the warm-up is not the exponential: the energy balance is separated and the time to a given rise is a numerical quadrature, with the temperature after a time found by root-finding (Illinois) and the time to the limit as the quadrature up to the allowed rise. A first attempt with mpmath's ODE solver took minutes per case and was dropped for the quadrature; cases with more than ten time constants elapsed are redrawn because the integrand is then too close to its pole. The generator now takes about 45 seconds. The calculator agreed with every vector at the first run. 9 built-in cases (8 reference, 1 rejection). The same author wrote the engine and the vectors, so the independence is in the method.
- Library card TL-158 links to the calculator; guides linked: heating formulas, thermal time constant, busbar temperature rise. `tools/calc_scope.json` has a checked and not-checked entry. Not in `tools/team_review_approval.json`. Page short name "Enclosure Thermal Rise".

- Next item from the Technical Library audit Build list (TL-175 "Energy Savings Estimator", article "Energy Saving Opportunities in Motors, Lighting and Distribution", division Energy Management; audit status "Partial": `led-retrofit-payback` covers lighting only, with no general savings estimator). New calculator `energy-savings` in division 07 Power and Energy (existing division, so the division count does not change). It combines up to three measures: motors (a number of motors replaced, the shaft output of each at its average load, the existing and new efficiency at that load, running hours and cost per motor), lighting (fittings replaced, existing and new power, burning hours, a percentage of hours cut by controls on the new fittings, cost per fitting) and a distribution loss reduction (loss power before and after, hours, total cost), any of which can be left out. With the tariff, its yearly rise, the discount rate, the life, a grid emission factor and an optional flat maintenance saving it gives the energy saved by each measure and in total, the share of the covered equipment's energy that is saved, the first-year cost saved, the investment, the simple payback, the present value of the savings over the life (energy savings at year end with the tariff rising, maintenance flat, investment at the start), the net present value, the savings to investment ratio and the carbon dioxide avoided. Results that do not apply (a measure left out, no investment) are left out through the engine's `na` list. The present value uses the closed-form annuity with a separate branch when the tariff rise equals the discount rate.
- The default is four 15 kW motors from 90 to 94 percent efficiency at 6,000 h, 200 fittings from 120 to 50 W at 4,000 h with 10 percent hours cut by controls, and an 18 to 11 kW loss reduction at 8,000 h, with an 8 per kWh tariff rising 3 percent a year, a 10 percent discount rate and 10 years: 17,021 + 60,000 + 56,000 = 133,021 kWh a year (20.78 percent of the covered equipment's energy), 1,064,170 saved in the first year against 950,000 invested, a simple payback of 0.89 years, a present value of 7,325,475, a net present value of 6,375,475, a ratio of 7.71 and 93.1 t of CO₂ at 0.7 kg/kWh. The defaults are an illustration with a short payback, not a typical project, and the 0.7 kg/kWh emission factor is an example figure that the hint tells the user to replace. Warnings: new motors not more efficient than the old, savings of lighting or distribution that are not positive, no first-year saving, a payback longer than the life, and a negative net present value with a payback inside the life. Rejected: new motor efficiency not above the existing one, losses after not below losses before, and no measure included.
- 34 independent vectors in `tests/gen_vectors.py` (own random stream, earlier 5,728 unchanged), 30 random and 4 named (the default, motors only with a flat tariff and a zero discount rate, a tariff rise equal to the discount rate, and a negative net present value with a payback of four years), written as a different route from the engine's formulas, in exact rational arithmetic: the energy is a before total less an after total, the share saved is one less after over before, and the present value is the explicit year-by-year sum rather than the closed-form annuity. The calculator agreed with every vector at the first run. 11 built-in cases (8 reference, 3 rejection). The same author wrote the engine and the vectors, so the independence is in the method.

A rectangular array of identical luminaires (1 to 10 a side, each at the centre of its own rectangle) hangs at a stated height above a horizontal plane. Each has an intensity straight down in candela and a light spread exponent m, so that the intensity at the angle g from the vertical is I0 cos^m g (0 is equal in all downward directions, 1 a cosine pattern, larger numbers narrower beams). The illuminance at a point is the maintenance factor times the sum over the luminaires of I(g) cos g / d^2, which is MF I0 h^(m+1) / (r^2 + h^2)^((m+3)/2) for each. It is evaluated at the centres of up to 20 by 20 equal rectangles over the area, and gives the average, minimum and maximum, Uo = Emin / Eavg, U1 = Emin / Emax, the position of the darkest point, the illuminance at a chosen point, the illuminance under one luminaire alone, a comparison with the required average and Uo (the user's figures, not looked up), the lux the darkest point would need, the flux the pattern implies (2 pi I0 / (m + 1) each), the share of it that lands on the area, and the half-intensity angle.

Direct light only, so the area edge is dark; this is stated in the assumptions. Warnings: fewer calculation points than luminaires along a side, average below the requirement, uniformity below the requirement.

- Next item from the Technical Library audit Build list (TL-013 "Differential Equation Solver", article "First-Order Differential Equations in Transient Circuits", division Engineering Mathematics; audit status "Missing": no calculator and no guide). New calculator `first-order-ode` in division 37 Engineering Mathematics (existing division, so the division count does not change). It solves the first-order linear equation a dy/dt + b y = g(t) with y(0) given, for a step (g = F), a ramp (g = F t) or a sine (g = F sin ωt) forcing, with a and b above zero. It gives the time constant τ = a / b, the forced and transient parts at a chosen time, y(t), the slope dy/dt = (g − b y) / a, the forcing g(t), the transient size at t = 0, the final value F / b for a step, the steady-state amplitude and phase lag arctan(ωτ) for a sine, and the time for the transient to settle into a band of the user's choice (τ ln(100 / band)). It also integrates the equation with 4000 classical Runge-Kutta steps from 0 to t and reports the difference from the closed form, with a warning when it is above one part in a million. Results that do not apply (final value for a ramp or sine, amplitude and lag for a step or ramp) are left out through the engine's `na` list.
- The default is a = 2, b = 4, F = 12, y(0) = 0 at t = 1 (a step): τ = 0.5, final value 3, y(1) = 3(1 − e^−2) = 2.59399, slope 0.81201, 2% settling time 1.95601. Warnings: no transient when y(0) equals the forced part at 0, a ramp has no final value, and a Runge-Kutta difference above one part in a million. Rejected: a or b not above zero, a settling band at or outside 0 to 100, a negative time or ω.
- 66 independent vectors in `tests/gen_vectors.py` (own random stream, earlier 5,839 unchanged), 59 random and 7 named, written as a different route from the engine's closed forms with 30-digit mpmath: the equation is put in the form y′ + y / τ = g / a and solved by the integrating factor, y = e^(−t/τ) [y0 + (1/a) ∫ e^(s/τ) g(s) ds], with the integral taken by numerical quadrature (`mp.quad`) over 48 sub-intervals; the sine forced part at 0 is taken from the periodicity of the long-time solution, and the transient is what is left. The generator now takes about 100 seconds. The vectors found one defect on the first run: the engine returned a negative steady-state amplitude for a sine with a negative F. It now returns the magnitude (|F / b| / √(1 + ω²τ²)), and a built-in case and two named vectors cover it. Two named vectors whose true result is zero or very near zero (a sine read at t = π, a start at the final value) were changed so the comparison is not against rounding noise. The calculator agreed with every vector after that fix. 9 built-in cases (6 reference, 3 rejection) plus the shared unit checks. The same author wrote the engine and the vectors, so the independence is in the method.
- Library card TL-013 links to the calculator; guides linked: RC filters, capacitor discharge time, inductors, thermal time constants. `tools/calc_scope.json` has a checked and not-checked entry (one forcing at a time, constant positive coefficients, one consistent unit set, the Runge-Kutta line is a cross-check). Stage 3: classified Not applicable in `tools/standard_applicability.json` (textbook first-order linear differential equation), a scope screen to confirm. Not in `tools/team_review_approval.json`. Page short name "First-Order ODE Solver".

## v1.97.36

- Next item from the Technical Library audit Build list (TL-014 "Laplace Transform Reference Tool", article "Laplace Transform: Electrical Engineering Applications", division Engineering Mathematics; audit status "Missing": no calculator and no guide). New calculator `laplace-transform` in division 37 Engineering Mathematics (existing division, so the division count does not change). It evaluates the one-sided Laplace transform of ten standard functions times an amplitude K (unit step, ramp, tⁿ, e^(−at), t e^(−at), tⁿ e^(−at), sin ωt, cos ωt, e^(−at) sin ωt, e^(−at) cos ωt) with an optional time delay T, at a point s = σ + jΩ: real and imaginary parts, magnitude and phase of F(s), the abscissa of convergence, the time function at a chosen time, the initial value f(0+) and the final value (only where it exists, through the engine's `na` list). The delay multiplies the table result by e^(−sT) (second shifting theorem). It also integrates K f(t − T) e^(−st) numerically (Simpson, 60 / (σ − abscissa) seconds, at least 120 points per cycle) and reports the difference from the table value, with a warning above one part in 100 000.
- The default is e^(−2t) sin 3t (K = 1) at s = 3 + j2: F = 3 / ((5 + j2)² + 9) = 3 / (30 + j20) = 0.069231 − j0.046154, magnitude 0.083205, phase −33.690°, abscissa −2, f(0.5) = 0.366958, f(0+) = 0, final value 0. Warnings: a delay makes the initial value 0, the ramp, tⁿ, sine and cosine have no final value (the final value theorem fails for them), σ within 0.05 of the abscissa, and a check that disagrees. Rejected: σ at or below the abscissa (0, or −a for the exponential forms), K = 0, a non-integer power n, a or ω not above zero, a negative delay or time.
- 66 independent vectors in `tests/gen_vectors.py` (own random stream, earlier 5,905 unchanged), 56 random and 10 named, written as a different route from the table pairs with 30-digit mpmath: F(s) is the defining integral of the delayed function from T, taken by numerical quadrature (`mp.quad`) over one-period sub-intervals, so neither the table nor the shifting theorem is used; the initial value is f read just after t = 0 and the final value comes from f at a very large time. The generator takes about 100 seconds. Two faults were found and fixed on the first run: the vector generator read the initial value of a delayed function just after the delay instead of just after t = 0 (a generator fault), and the engine returned a phase of −180° instead of 180° for a negative amplitude on the real axis because of a signed zero in the imaginary part (an engine fault; the parts are now normalised). The calculator agreed with every vector after that. 12 built-in cases (8 reference, 4 rejection) plus the shared unit checks. The same author wrote the engine and the vectors, so the independence is in the method.
- Library card TL-014 links to the calculator; guides linked: RC filters, inductors, resonance, thermal time constants. `tools/calc_scope.json` has a checked and not-checked entry (ten pairs only, one delay, no inverse transform of a general ratio, not a frequency response). Stage 3: classified Not applicable in `tools/standard_applicability.json` (textbook Laplace transform), a scope screen to confirm. Not in `tools/team_review_approval.json`. Page short name "Laplace Transform Table".

## v1.97.37

- Next item from the Technical Library audit Build list (TL-028 "Electric Field Calculator", article "Electric Field Intensity, Potential and Capacitance", division Electromagnetic Engineering; audit status "Missing": no calculator and no guide). New calculator `electric-field` in division 04 DC Circuits beside `electric-potential` (existing division, so the division count does not change). It finds the electric field at a point in a plane from one, two or three point charges in a medium of relative permittivity εr: the distance and field strength from each charge, the net field as x and y components, its magnitude (V/m) and direction (degrees from the +x axis), and the force on a test charge (q0 E, reversed for a negative q0) with its direction. The fields are added as vectors. Results that do not apply (unused charges, and the directions when the net field is zero or the test charge is zero) are left out through the engine's `na` list.
- The default is 4, −2 and 3 µC at (0, 0), (0.4, 0) and (0, 0.3) m with the field point at (0.2, 0.15) m, 0.25 m from each: E1 = 575,203 V/m, E2 = 287,602 V/m, E3 = 431,402 V/m, net Ex = 1,035,366 V/m, Ey = −86,280 V/m, E = 1,038,955 V/m at −4.764°, force on 1 µC 1.03895 N. Warning: where the fields cancel (zero field is not zero potential). Rejected: the field point on a charge, two charges at one position, εr below 1.
- 39 independent vectors in `tests/gen_vectors.py` (own random stream, earlier 5,971 unchanged), 34 random and 5 named, written as a different route from the engine's vector sum with 30-digit mpmath: the field is minus the gradient of the potential, E = −∇V, found by numerical differentiation (`mp.diff`), with each charge's own field the length of its own potential gradient; k comes from μ0 c² / 4π. The calculator agreed with every vector on the first run. 9 built-in cases (6 reference, 3 rejection) plus the shared unit checks. The same author wrote the engine and the vectors, so the independence is in the method. The force is shown as a plain number labelled in newtons because the registry's unit table has no force dimension.
- Library card TL-028 links to the calculator; guides linked: Coulomb's law, capacitors, parallel plate capacitors. `tools/calc_scope.json` has a checked and not-checked entry (point charges only, one plane, no breakdown or corona check). Stage 3: classified Not applicable in `tools/standard_applicability.json` (textbook electrostatics), a scope screen to confirm. Not in `tools/team_review_approval.json`. ## v1.98.0

- Next item from the Technical Library audit Build list (TL-056 "Reliability Indices Calculator", article "Power System Reliability: SAIDI, SAIFI and Availability", division Power Systems; audit status "Missing": no calculator and no guide). New calculator `reliability-indices` in division 19 Power Systems (existing division, so the division count does not change). It works out the usual distribution reliability indices from a list of up to five sustained interruption events: customer interruptions and customer-minutes lost, SAIFI = Σ Ci / NT, SAIDI = Σ Ci ri / NT, CAIDI = SAIDI / SAIFI, ASAI and ASUI over a reporting period, the energy not supplied (and per customer) from one average load per interrupted customer, the largest single event's share of SAIDI and which event it is, and a check of SAIFI and SAIDI against targets the user enters. Results that do not apply (CAIDI with no interruptions, the energy with a zero load, the largest-event share with nothing lost) are left out through the engine's `na` list.
- The default is 20,000 customers over 8,760 h with five events (1,200 for 90 min, 450 for 240 min, 3,000 for 45 min, 800 for 120 min, 150 for 360 min): 5,600 customer interruptions, 501,000 customer-minutes, SAIFI 0.28, SAIDI 25.05 min, CAIDI 89.46 min, ASAI 99.99523 %, energy not supplied 12,525 kWh at 1.5 kW per customer (0.626 kWh per customer), with event 3 the largest at 26.95 % of SAIDI. Warnings: SAIFI or SAIDI above a target, and any event under 5 minutes (IEEE Std 1366 treats it as momentary; here every event entered is counted). Rejected: more customers interrupted than served, an event longer than the period, customers interrupted with no duration, and customer-time lost above what all customers could lose in the period.
- 35 independent vectors in `tests/gen_vectors.py` (own random stream, earlier 6,010 unchanged), 30 random and 5 named, written as a different route from the engine in exact fractions: indices built from fractions of customers and hours (SAIFI as the sum of Ci / NT, CAIDI as the mean event length weighted by Ci / ΣCi, ASAI from customer-hours) instead of the engine's customer-seconds. The calculator agreed with every vector on the first run. 9 built-in cases (6 reference, 3 rejection) plus the shared unit checks. The same author wrote the engine and the vectors, so the independence is in the method.
- Library card TL-056 links to the calculator; guides linked: demand and load factor, energy and cost, UPS. `tools/calc_scope.json` has a checked and not-checked entry (no IEEE Std 1366 momentary or major-event-day exclusion, no MAIFI, ASIFI or ASIDI, no regulator targets). Stage 3: classified Not applicable in `tools/standard_applicability.json` (definitions of reporting indices, no clause review), a scope screen to confirm. Not in `tools/team_review_approval.json`. Page short name "Reliability Indices".

- Next item from the Technical Library audit Build list (TL-091 "SCR Triggering Calculator", article on SCR triggering, division Electronics Engineering; audit status "Missing"). New calculator `scr-triggering` in division 24 Semiconductor Components beside `controlled-rectifier`. Mode 1 designs a DC or pulse gate drive from the data-sheet VGT and IGT: gate current (margin x IGT), largest series gate resistor, gate and resistor power, duty and average gate power, with warnings above PGM and PG(AV). Mode 2 gives the firing angle of a resistor-triggered half-wave AC circuit (asin of the firing voltage over the supply peak, 0 to 90 degrees only), the conduction angle, average and RMS resistive load voltage and the largest gate resistance that still fires; a supply that never reaches the firing voltage is flagged.
- Default (mode 1): 12 V source, VGT 1 V, IGT 15 mA, margin 2, 100 us pulse every 10 ms: 30 mA, Rmax 366.67 ohm, gate power 0.03 W, average 0.3 mW. Mode 2 example: 230 V, 10 kohm, IGT 15 mA, VD 0.7 V gives a firing voltage of 151.7 V and a firing angle of about 27.8 degrees. Rejected: source not above VGT, pulse longer than the period, supply peak not above VGT plus the diode drop. Not checked: IGM, latching and holding current, dv/dt, di/dt, RC phase shift, inductive loads.

The same author wrote the engine and the vectors, so the independence is in the method. Caught while building: the vectors failed the first run because the engine's half-wave RMS load voltage used 2π instead of 4π under the root (a factor of √2 too high); the engine was fixed, not the vectors.

## v1.98.2

- Next item from the Technical Library audit Build list (TL-103 "PID Tuning Calculator", article "PID Controllers: Proportional, Integral and Derivative Actions", division Control Systems; audit status "Missing": no calculator and no guide). New calculator `pid-tuning` in division 03 AC Circuits beside `second-order-response` and `routh-hurwitz` (existing division, so the division count does not change). Five methods and three controller types (P, PI, PID). From a first-order-plus-dead-time process (gain K, time constant, dead time): Ziegler-Nichols reaction curve, Cohen-Coon, and Lambda / IMC for a closed-loop time constant you choose. From an ultimate gain and period: Ziegler-Nichols and Tyreus-Luyben. It gives Kc, Ti, Td, the parallel-form Ki = Kc / Ti and Kd = Kc x Td, the proportional band and, for the model methods, theta / tau.
- Default (method 1, PID): K 2, tau 50 s, theta 10 s gives Kc 3, Ti 20 s, Td 5 s, Ki 0.15, Kd 15, proportional band 33.33 %. Warnings: theta / tau above 1 or below 0.1 for the reaction-curve and Cohen-Coon rules, and a lambda shorter than the dead time. Rejected: a P controller for Lambda and Tyreus-Luyben (they have no proportional-only form). Not checked: fit of the model to the real process, closed-loop stability, overshoot or margins, interacting PID forms, derivative filtering, anti-windup, integrating or unstable processes, dead-time compensators.
- 66 independent vectors in `tests/gen_vectors.py` (own random stream, earlier 6,084 unchanged). The same author wrote the engine and the vectors, so the independence is in the method: reaction-curve rules from the reaction rate R = K / tau, Cohen-Coon in common-denominator form, ultimate-gain rules as exact fractions with Ku and Pu found by root-finding the phase crossover of the process; Lambda PID by pole-zero cancellation and a closed-loop pole at -1 / lambda in exact fractions; Lambda PI by root-finding the gain that gives unity loop gain at 1 / (lambda + theta). 18 built-in cases.
- Library card TL-103 links to the calculator; guides linked: thermal time constant, RC filter, resonance. `tools/calc_scope.json` has a checked and not-checked entry. Stage 3: classified Not applicable in `tools/standard_applicability.json` (published empirical tuning recipes, no governing standard), a scope screen to confirm. Not in `tools/team_review_approval.json`, so the reviewer-designation check for the new calculator is left for the owner.

## v1.98.3

- Next item from the Technical Library audit Build list (TL-104 "Bode Plot Calculator", division Control Systems; audit status "Missing": no calculator and no guide). New calculator `bode-plot` in division 03 AC Circuits beside `pid-tuning`, `second-order-response` and `routh-hurwitz` (existing division, so the division count does not change). It takes an open-loop transfer function in Bode form: gain K, up to two integrators, up to two zeros, up to three real poles (corner frequencies in rad/s) and an optional dead time. At a chosen frequency it gives the exact magnitude (linear and dB) and phase, the straight-line (asymptotic) magnitude, its slope and the exact-minus-straight-line difference. It also finds the first gain crossover and phase margin, the first phase crossover and gain margin, and flags negative or small margins.
- Default: 10 / (s (1 + s)(1 + s/10)) at 1 rad/s gives 16.946 dB and -140.71 degrees; gain crossover 3.0145 rad/s with a phase margin of 1.58 degrees, phase crossover 3.1623 rad/s with a gain margin of 0.83 dB (both small, so the calculator warns). Rejected inputs: K, frequency or a corner below zero. Not checked: complex pole or zero pairs, right-half-plane poles or zeros, the Nyquist criterion, crossovers after the first, the closed-loop response.
- 60 independent vectors in `tests/gen_vectors.py` (own random stream, earlier 6,150 unchanged). The same author wrote the engine and the vectors, so the independence is in the method: the response is built as a complex number from numerator and denominator polynomials and read with abs() and arg(); the crossovers are roots of polynomials (mp.polyroots), not a scan. 8 built-in cases.
- Library card TL-104 links to the calculator; `tools/calc_scope.json` entry added; classified Not applicable in `tools/standard_applicability.json` (scope screen to confirm). Not in `tools/team_review_approval.json`.

## v1.98.4

- Next item from the Technical Library audit Build list (TL-127 "EV Range Estimator", article "EV Battery Pack Voltage, Capacity and Range Estimation", division Electric Vehicles; audit status "Missing": no calculator and no guide). New calculator `ev-range` in division 27 EV Charging beside `ev-energy-consumption` and `ev-charge-time` (existing division, so the division count does not change). It builds the pack from the cells in series and parallel and the cell voltage and capacity (pack voltage, pack capacity, number of cells, nominal energy), scales the energy by a state of health and a temperature capacity factor you enter, takes the usable energy between a starting charge and a reserve, and divides it by the total consumption (driving Wh/km plus the auxiliary power spread over the average speed) to give the range in km and miles. It also gives the range from 100% to 0%, the range of a new pack with no auxiliary load and the share lost, distance per 1% of charge, driving time, average battery power and discharge rate, and for a trip distance the energy, the charge needed, the charge on arrival and the distance margin.
- The default is 96S2P of 3.7 V 100 Ah cells (355.2 V, 200 Ah, 71.04 kWh), 95% health, a 90% temperature factor, 95% start and 10% reserve, 160 Wh/km driving and 1 kW auxiliary at 80 km/h: available 60.74 kWh, usable 51.63 kWh, total 172.5 Wh/km, range 299.29 km (185.97 miles), 32.59% below the 444 km of a new pack with no auxiliary load, 3.52 km per 1%, 13.8 kW (0.227 C), and a 250 km trip needs 71.0% and arrives at 24.0%. Warnings: a trip that arrives below the reserve, a cell voltage outside 2 to 4 V, a pack outside 100 to 1,000 V, a state of health below 70%, a temperature factor outside 70 to 100%, a consumption outside 100 to 400 Wh/km and a discharge rate above 1 C. Rejected: a reserve at or above the starting charge, a fractional cell count, a zero speed or consumption.
- 38 independent vectors in `tests/gen_vectors.py` (own random stream, earlier 6,210 unchanged), 32 random and 6 named, written as a different route from the engine's closed forms in exact fractions: the pack is built cell by cell (voltage summed along a series string, capacity summed over the parallel branches, nominal energy summed over every cell as voltage times capacity), and the vehicle is driven one kilometre at a time, each km taking the driving Wh plus the auxiliary power times the hours that km takes, with the whole kilometres counted while the usable energy lasts and the last part-kilometre taken as the remainder. The engine agreed with every vector on the first run. The same author wrote the engine and the vectors, so the independence is in the method. 9 built-in cases. Caught while building: the first version gave the cell voltage as a voltage input, which triggered the generic "below 12 V" unit warning; it is now a plain number, and the miles figure is a number because the length table has no mile unit.
- Library card TL-127 links to the calculator; guides linked: EV charging time, battery capacity, C-rate, Peukert's law, battery storage efficiency. `tools/calc_scope.json` has a checked and not-checked entry (no cell voltage curve, no rate or temperature model, no battery-management buffer, no test-cycle range). Stage 3: classified Not applicable in `tools/standard_applicability.json` (textbook pack arithmetic, no governing standard), a scope screen to confirm. Not in `tools/team_review_approval.json`.

## v1.98.5

- Next item from the Technical Library audit Build list (TL-160 "Wavelength Calculator", article "Electromagnetic Waves and Their Engineering Applications", division Engineering Physics; audit status "Missing": no calculator and no guide). New calculator `wavelength` in division 03 AC Circuits beside `frequency-period` and `resonance-lc` (existing division, so the division count does not change). It finds the wavelength from a frequency, or the frequency from a wavelength, for a plane wave in a medium given either by its relative permittivity and permeability (speed c / sqrt(er mur)) or by a velocity factor (cables and lines). It gives the speed of the wave, velocity factor, refractive index, the wavelength in the medium and in free space, the period, the wave number, the half-wave and quarter-wave lengths and, for the permittivity and permeability way of entering the medium, the wave impedance eta0 sqrt(mur / er). A physical length (antenna element, cable, stub, trace) is also expressed as a fraction of a wavelength and in degrees. GHz was added to the frequency units of the calculators (the Conversion Centre already had it).
- Default: 100 MHz in free space, wavelength 2.99792458 m, half-wave 1.49896 m, quarter-wave 0.749481 m, period 0.01 us, wave number 2.0958 rad/m, wave impedance 376.7303 ohm, and a 1.5 m length is 0.5003 of a wavelength (180.1 degrees), which is flagged as long enough to behave as a transmission line (more than one tenth of a wavelength). Other warnings: a refractive index above 10 and a frequency above 300 GHz. Rejected: a relative permittivity below 1, a velocity factor above 1 (or 0 or less), a zero or negative frequency, wavelength or permeability. Not checked: loss, dispersion and the change of permittivity with frequency and temperature, the characteristic impedance of a line, reflections, skin depth, waveguides, antenna end effect.
- 38 independent vectors in `tests/gen_vectors.py` (own random stream, earlier 6,248 unchanged), 32 random and 6 named, in 40-digit mpmath by a different route from the engine's c / sqrt(er mur): the medium is built from mu = mu0 mur and eps = eps0 er with eps0 = 1 / (mu0 c^2) (CODATA 2018 mu0), the wave is exp(j (w t - k z)) with k = w sqrt(mu eps), the wavelength is the distance over which the phase falls by 2 pi, the wave impedance is sqrt(mu / eps), a velocity factor is a medium with k = w / (VF c), a frequency from a wavelength comes from k = 2 pi / lambda, and the electrical length is the phase shift k L in radians turned into degrees. The engine agreed with every vector on the first run. The same author wrote the engine and the vectors, so the independence is in the method. 12 built-in cases.
- Library card TL-160 links to the calculator; guides linked: transmission lines, resonance, AC circuits, skin effect. `tools/calc_scope.json` has a checked and not-checked entry. ## v1.98.6

- Next item from the Technical Library audit Build list (TL-165 "Contact Resistance Calculator", article "Electrical Contacts and Connectors: Resistance, Heating and Reliability", division Electrical Materials; audit status "Missing": no calculator and no guide). New calculator `contact-resistance` in division 29 Industrial Electrical beside `busbar-sizing` (existing division, so the division count does not change). Mode 1 finds the resistance of a contact from the contact force and the hardness of the softer member: true contact area A = F / H, the radius of each of n equal spots, the Holm constriction resistance (rho1 + rho2) / (4 n a) and a surface film entered as a resistance per unit area (mOhm mm2) in series, giving the total contact resistance. Mode 2 takes a measured millivolt drop and test current, gives the resistance and power, and the radius of the single clean spot that would give that resistance (also shown in mode 1, where it shows what a film or several spots do). Both give the voltage drop and power loss at the current, the temperature of the hottest spot above the bulk metal (Kohlrausch relation, Lorenz number 2.45e-8 V2/K2), the contact voltage and current at which the spot reaches a softening and a melting temperature the user enters, and the total resistance as a multiple of a limit the user enters.
- Default: 100 N on copper (1.68e-8 ohm m, 1,000 MPa), one spot: area 0.1 mm2, radius 178.41 um, resistance 47.08 uOhm, 4.708 mV and 0.471 W at 100 A, hottest spot 0.37 K above the bulk metal (30.37 C), softening at 109.6 mV (2,328 A) and melting at 414.4 mV (8,803 A). Warnings: a contact voltage at or above the softening voltage, and at or above the melting voltage (the spot temperature is then not a real value), a resistance above the user's limit, a resistivity outside 1e-8 to 1e-6 ohm m, a hardness outside 50 to 6,000 MPa and a bulk temperature at or above the softening temperature. Rejected: a fractional number of spots, a softening temperature not above the bulk temperature, a melting temperature not above the softening temperature, and a zero or negative force, hardness, resistivity or current. Not checked: the real pattern, spacing and interaction of contact spots (equal, far-apart spots make the clean-metal figure a lower bound), roughness, plating thickness, elastic contact, creep, relaxation, fretting and wear, the temperature rise of the whole joint, AC effects, and any acceptance limit or test method from a standard. No hardness or film values are embedded: they are the user's data, with typical ranges in the hints.
- 38 independent vectors in `tests/gen_vectors.py` (own random stream, earlier 6,286 unchanged), 32 random and 6 named, in 40-digit mpmath by a different route from the engine's closed forms: the half-space spreading resistance is the numerical integral rho / (2 pi a) times the integral of du / (1 + u^2), the spot radius is found by a root finder on n pi a^2 H = F, n spots are summed as parallel conductances, the single-spot radius by bisection on ln a, and the spot temperature and softening and melting voltages by root finding on the Kohlrausch relation (the Lorenz number and the relation itself are shared, so those results are independent in method only). The first run found one real issue: the temperature rise was computed as a difference of two nearly equal temperatures and lost digits at a few microvolts (relative error 1.8e-8, above the 1e-8 vector tolerance). The engine now computes (T - Tb) = (U2 / 4L) / (T + Tb) and the voltage at a temperature from (T - Tb)(T + Tb); no other result changed. The same author wrote the engine and the vectors, so the independence is in the method. 14 built-in cases.
- Library card TL-165 links to the calculator; guides linked: conductor resistance, busbar temperature rise, heating, resistors. `tools/calc_scope.json` has a checked and not-checked entry. Not in `tools/team_review_approval.json`. Page short name "Contact Resistance".

## v1.98.7

- Next item from the Technical Library audit Build list (TL-193 "Signage Load Calculator", article "Airport Signs and Airfield Electrical Signage Systems", division Infrastructure Engineering; audit status "Missing": no calculator and no guide; the library article is still "Not started", so the scope comes from the card title and the existing AGL series-circuit calculators). New calculator `signage-load` in division 35 Airport and AGL beside `agl-ccr-loading` (existing division, so the division count does not change). You enter up to four groups of airfield guidance signs (number of signs, lamp load of one sign at the circuit current, rating of the isolating transformer that feeds one sign), one isolating transformer efficiency, the circuit current, the series cable loop length and resistance per kilometre, any other load on the same circuit, a spare capacity for future signs as a percentage of the sign load, and a maximum CCR loading. It gives the lamp load and the load at the transformer inputs for each group and for all the signs, the cable loss (I2 R L), the total circuit load and the CCR output voltage, each group's lamp load as a share of its transformer rating and the most heavily loaded group (the first, on a tie), the design load with the spare capacity (sign load only; cable loss and other load unchanged), the smallest standard CCR (1, 2, 4, 7.5, 10, 15, 20, 25, 30 kW, the list `agl-ccr-loading` uses) that holds the design load at the maximum loading, its loading now and at the design load, the room left at the maximum loading and how many more signs of the average size fit. A group with no signs is left out of the group outputs through the `na` list; above 30 kW the CCR outputs are left out and the circuit is flagged for splitting.
- Default: 10 signs of 100 W on 150 W transformers, 6 of 200 W on 300 W, 4 of 250 W on 300 W and 2 of 60 W on 100 W, efficiency 0.85, 6.6 A, 3,500 m at 3.08 ohm/km, no other load, 20% spare, 80% loading: 22 signs, 3.320 kW of lamp, 3.906 kW at the transformer inputs, 469.6 W of cable loss, 4.375 kW and 663 V in all, the most heavily loaded transformer 83.3% (group 3), design load 5.157 kW, a 7.5 kW CCR 58.3% loaded now and 68.8% at the design load, 1.625 kW of room and 9 more signs of the average 177.5 W. Warnings: a lamp load above its transformer rating (the transformer would be overloaded) and a design load above 30 kW at the chosen loading. Rejected: a fractional number of signs, no signs in any group, a group with signs and a zero lamp load or transformer rating, and the shared range checks (efficiency 0.5 to 1, loading 0.3 to 1). Not checked: sign type, size or class, the number of lamps per sign, lamp load at other brightness steps, sign heaters, transformer thermal or open-circuit behaviour, lamp failure, CCR output curves and power factor, sign siting and circuit layout, and any requirement of ICAO Annex 14 or the FAA advisory circulars. No lamp load, transformer rating or efficiency is embedded: all are the user's data.
- 39 independent vectors in `tests/gen_vectors.py` (own random stream, earlier 6,324 unchanged), 32 random and 7 named, written as a different route from the engine's group sums in exact fractions: every sign is its own load in a list, the loop is a resistance in ohms and the loss is I2 times it, the total is a sum over the list, the CCR is the first size in a scan whose loading limit is at or above the design load, the number of further signs is counted by adding average signs one at a time, and the most heavily loaded transformer is found by cross-multiplying instead of dividing; random cases keep the design load and the room away from a size or sign-count edge. The calculator agreed with every vector at the first run. 13 built-in cases (8 reference, 5 rejection) plus the shared unit checks (63 in all); their expected numbers come from an exact-fraction reference written separately from the engine. The same author wrote the engine and the vectors, so the independence is in the method.

## v1.98.8

- Next item from the Technical Library audit Build list (TL-064 "Protection Coordination Checker", article "Protection Selectivity, Sensitivity and Stability Explained", division Protection Engineering; audit status "Guide only": a static guide exists, no interactive calculator). New calculator `protection-coordination-check` in division 16 Protection beside `relay-setting` and `relay-coordination` (existing division, so the division count does not change). It checks an overcurrent relay on a feeder against the three tests of the article. Stability: the lowest pick-up that stays stable on the maximum load (load margin times the load current divided by the reset ratio), the load stability margin of the pick-up entered, and, for an optional instantaneous element, its setting as a multiple of the largest through fault against the factor you require. Sensitivity: the highest pick-up that meets a sensitivity factor on the smallest fault at the far end of the zone, and the sensitivity factor of the pick-up entered; the instantaneous reach is the smallest fault as a multiple of the instantaneous setting. Selectivity: a downstream and an upstream relay on the IEC 60255-151 standard, very, extremely or long-time inverse curve (the constants `relay-coordination` uses), graded at the largest through fault and at the smallest fault against the grading margin you want. It also gives the number of checks made and failed. `relay-coordination` finds the upstream TMS for one fault; this one checks settings you already have against all three tests.
- Default: 180 A load, margin 1.1, 250 A pick-up, reset ratio 0.95, 1,100 A smallest fault, sensitivity factor 1.5, 6,000 A largest through fault, 7,500 A instantaneous at factor 1.2, standard inverse relays (downstream TMS 0.1, upstream 400 A at TMS 0.25) and a 0.4 s margin: the pick-up window is 208.42 to 733.33 A, the load stability margin 1.1995, the sensitivity factor 4.4, the instantaneous factor 1.25 (reach 0.1467, so the smallest fault is below it and a warning says the time-delayed element covers the far end), the downstream relay takes 0.21333 s and the upstream relay 0.62888 s at 6,000 A (margin 0.41554 s) and 0.46550 s and 1.71249 s at 1,100 A (margin 1.24699 s); 5 checks made, none failed. Warnings: a pick-up below the lowest stable setting, a sensitivity factor below the one required (and one at or below 1, where the relay does not pick up), no pick-up satisfying both tests, an instantaneous setting below the required factor (or at or below the through fault), a margin below the one wanted (or the upstream relay not slower), an upstream pick-up not above the downstream one, and a smallest fault below the upstream pick-up (no back-up; the smallest-fault grading is then left out through the `na` list). Rejected: a largest through fault below the smallest fault or not above both pick-ups, an instantaneous setting at or below the pick-up, and the shared range checks (reset ratio 0.5 to 1, margins of at least 1). Not checked: CT saturation, DC offset and decrement, earth-fault, directional, differential and thermal elements, cable and transformer withstand, the relay maker's own curves and reset times, infeeds, and any utility rule or standard value for the margins: they are your inputs.
- 39 independent vectors in `tests/gen_vectors.py` (own random stream, earlier 6,363 unchanged), 32 random and 7 named. The window, the stability, sensitivity and instantaneous factors and each pass or fail are exact fractions compared by cross-multiplication; the operating time is the root of an induction-disc travel integral with the rate built from expm1(alpha ln M) in 40-digit mpmath, so the timing is independent in arithmetic only (the IEC curve form is shared with the engine). Random cases keep every check at least 1% from its pass or fail edge; an expected zero (no failed checks) is left out because the comparison is relative. The calculator agreed with every vector at the first run. 12 built-in cases (7 reference, 1 no-window and 4 rejection), their expected numbers from the Python reference. The same author wrote the engine and the vectors, so the independence is in the method.
- Library card TL-064 links to the calculator; guides linked: overcurrent relay curves, CT burden, short-circuit level, circuit breakers. `tools/calc_scope.json` has a checked and not-checked entry. Stage 3: a `scope_screened` record in `tools/review_records.json` under IEC 60255-151 (2009 Ed 1.0), the same standard and edition as the `relay-coordination` record; its basis says it reuses that public scope screen and was not re-checked against the publisher catalogue for this calculator, and no licensed clause text was reviewed. It is a scope screen to confirm, not a clause review. Not in `tools/team_review_approval.json`. Page short name "Protection Coordination Checker".
